Finite element model updating is among the most general approaches to discover constitutive models from structural experiments: since the displacement field is computed rather than measured, it accommodates arbitrary geometries, boundary conditions, and sparse or noisy data. For a two-phase specimen, training time decreases from nine hours to 41 minutes compared with a full-order identification, without loss of accuracy. In the three-dimensional example, six modes replace 48 672 degrees of freedom, 116 of 15 625 elements suffice to evaluate the internal forces, and training takes 13 minutes. In all cases, the identified material reproduces the measured reaction force and predicts the displacement field from the force alone. Hyperreduction makes the discovery of neural materials from structural data affordable while retaining the advantages of finite element model updating.

We then present OSTEON, a discipline for the regime, released with a DOI (10.5281/zenodo.23436020): each project gets a governed knowledge repository of decisions and contracts, every change is checked mechanically against it, and the knowledge itself is checked for decay at every session start; planning is gated, and sessions record what they learn without writing rules. ##### **PriMa-Causa: Causal Prescriptive Maintenance**
2610.31147v1 by Felix Saretzky

Automated manufacturing requires data-driven decisions under uncertainty and sparse observational records, and unplanned downtime is a dominant cost of getting them wrong. Prescriptive maintenance recommends a maintenance action against an impending failure rather than merely predicting it. Established reference architectures couple predictive models with rule- or optimisation-based recommendation logic. Selecting a corrective action, however, requires an interventional quantity: how the line behaviour would change if the action were set. Manufacturing data is observational, so a model fitted to predict the outcome estimates an associational quantity and may misdirect the choice of action without the prediction error revealing it. Causal estimators target the interventional quantity but require an expert-led causal study per case, for which the bounded decision window of operation leaves no room. This dissertation analyses how causal reasoning can be embedded into prescriptive maintenance so that actions are ranked by their estimated interventional effect. The result is PriMa-Causa, built from three components: a causal reasoning layer that integrates the estimate into the PriMa reference model, a parametric simulator that supplies the interventional ground truth absent from manufacturing data, and a graph-conditioned tabular causal foundation model that, pre-trained once on this synthetic production-line prior, answers interventional queries in a single forward pass. PriMa-Causa attains a lower prescription error than associational baselines, with the largest margin under weak overlap and sparse context, and a partially disclosed causal graph reduces this error further. The causal inductive bias of the synthetic prior and the graph conditioning can thus replace per-case estimator fitting, a step toward automating causal inference for autonomous and self-healing maintenance.

We design a simple task to ask which model has the most influence on the final consensus when several models are mixed in one system. Experimentally, across seven model families, we find that the opinions of the other models are absorbed into the opinion of the stronger model, the one with more parameters. This happens even though the task requires no pretraining knowledge and has no correct answer. To understand this phenomenon, we interpret the agents as weighted particles moving on a simplex. Larger models carry a larger weight and therefore occupy a deeper well in the energy landscape. A large weight means strong in-context learning: a model with a large weight amplifies the majority of its context. Larger models are generally better at in-context learning, and this is what makes the larger model the influencer. We hope that this work helps to interpret multi-agent systems, whose behavior is becoming too complex for humans to understand.

##### **RIFT: KV Cache Compression for Draft Models with Complementary Compressibility Training**
2610.31098v1 by Dor Tsur, Maor Ashkenazi, Maximilian Golub, Peter Hicks, Yaniv Galron, Talor Abramovich, Yoav Miron, Tiyasa Mitra, Bita Darvish Rouhani, Ran Zilberstein

Speculative decoding is a popular decode acceleration method. As such, it is particularly relevant to long-output generation, which requires an increasingly large KV cache. KV cache compression offers a way to constrain costs in long-context drafting. However, compression methods that focus on prefill information can lose relevance as the output length grows. We term this phenomenon decode staleness and show that it degrades draft model accuracy under selection-based KV cache compression. We introduce RIFT, a family of selection-based KV cache compression strategies that balance prefill information and recent generated tokens. We show that refreshing the KV cache selection during token generation significantly improves the drafter's quality at the cost of additional decode latency. To alleviate the refresh costs, we propose an adaptive KV refresh strategy that reduces the number of score calculations while retaining the drafter's quality. We further demonstrate how a compressibility-driven drafter training phase, based on sparse attention masks, can improve the drafter's quality under a compressed KV cache. Combined with compressibility training, RIFT enables Qwen3.5-4B, drafting for Qwen3.5-122B, to retain over 98% of its full-cache acceptance length on 32K-token inputs, 4K-token outputs, and an 8K-token KV cache budget.

##### **Co-Recursive Semantic Self-Extension: A Mathematical Foundation for Open-Ended Intelligence**
2610.31097v1 by Simon Hegelich, Bardh Prenkaj, Habiba Sarhan, Morteza Shahrezaye, Kolja Hegelich, Eric Cambria, Christian Montag

Artificial intelligence has advanced through scale, data, and computation, yet remains bounded by its representational framework. We ask what makes intelligence open-ended. Every inferential system selects meaningful distinctions, limiting what it can learn. Within a fixed purpose, performance reaches a ceiling set by its semantic domain. Teleological succession, in which purposes arise from earlier ones, faces a similar limit: Boolean-closed purpose-transformations exhaust their reachable semantic territory. We shift from purposes to inferential systems. Drawing on Kant and Gödel, we model cognitive states as partial evaluations over a formal language. Unresolved propositions may be undecided or undecidable. Reasoning can resolve the former but not the latter, leaving a frontier for extension. Non-trivial reductions of undecidable propositions expose undecidable intermediate premises whose reflective reification supports multiple decisive extensions. Finite trace-guided generation remains fallible, requiring meta-level selection and polycontextural exploration. Using Kolmogorov complexity, we show that no architecture with a fixed state description, transition mechanism, and initial condition can generate histories incorporating irreducible algorithmic information. Because physical systems are fixed in this sense, we examine collectives. Coupling finitely many finite agents does not remove the bound. Extension requires persistent external memory preserving semantic content and historical coordinates. Collective generative capacity depends on balancing overlap for transfer with difference for generation. We propose semantic self-extension as a design principle: intelligence should not merely resolve ambiguity within existing meanings, but transform unresolved structures into new spaces of meaning, generating new distinctions, questions, representations, and purposes.

##### **What Pairwise Evaluation Misses: Reward Scores across Prompts**
2610.31090v1 by Mateusz Modrzejewski, Tomasz Radzikowski

Reward models score responses for preference optimization, filtering and ranking, and pooling scores across prompts needs them to be comparable. Yet pairwise evaluation compares two responses to the same prompt, so any score component shared by a prompt's responses cancels and goes untested. We study this component by comparing between-prompt variation in model scores with the same variation in human ratings of the same responses. We propose a human-referenced audit, built from a between-prompt variance share, a sibling-rating regression and prompt substitutions, and apply it to eighteen released scorers and six matched training conditions. We find that scores cluster by prompt more than the ratings do: on HelpSteer2, six reward models have between-prompt shares of 0.34 to 0.64 against a human share near zero. Replacing the original prompt with a fixed instruction keeps substantial clustering but weakens agreement with prompt-level human ratings, so clustering alone does not show agreement with humans. Part of that variation is useful: subtracting prompt means lowers the helpfulness of globally selected responses, and scores stay sensitive to a wrong prompt even when training shuffles prompt means. We propose that reward evaluation check between-prompt variation, sensitivity to a wrong prompt and usefulness for selection separately.

##### **MQAScore: Fine-Grained Text-Music Alignment Evaluation via Large Audio Language Model**
2610.31083v1 by Wei-Jaw Lee, Chun-Ping Wang, Xuanjun Chen, Yi-Hsuan Yang

Text-to-music alignment is commonly evaluated by the global similarity between text and audio embeddings, which collapses all described attributes into a single score and obscures which ones are missing. We instead introduce MQAScore, which verifies each atomic musical concept along five clip-level dimensions (genre, instrument, mood/theme, vocal, and usage) by the probability that a large audio language model (LALM) answers affirmatively to a binary question. To assess evaluator reliability, we construct verification labels from presence/absence annotations and LLM-guided concept substitutions, and propose the Sensitivity-Invariance Ratio, which measures how selectively an evaluator responds to meaning-changing substitutions. Experiments show that concept-level scoring improves both question-answering and similarity-based evaluators, and MQAScore can be a more reliable and interpretable fine-grained complement to global similarity for evaluating text-to-music alignment.

##### **SCOPE: Compiling Natural-Language Policies into Neurosymbolic Predicate Programs**
2610.31062v1 by Arnon Mazza, Elad Levi

Policy enforcement for LLM agents has shifted from single-turn content moderation to compound, application-specific policies evaluated over multi-turn transcripts and tool-call trajectories. Fine-tuned guardrail models are efficient but degrade on compound policies, and while LLM-as-a-judge approaches achieve higher accuracy, they incur high inference cost and tend to produce unstable predictions. Neurosymbolic methods offer a natural alternative, decomposing the policy into narrow neural decisions composed under explicit logic. However, existing symbolic layers lack the required expressiveness and are optimized against fixed data rather than the policy itself. To address this, we introduce SCOPE, which compiles a policy into an executable program of neural predicates executed by small models, deterministic extractors, and code that composes their outputs. The program is optimized jointly with its training data, using adversarial generation of counterexamples that target where the program deviates from the policy. We evaluate SCOPE on a benchmark of real-world policy tasks against prompt optimization, task decomposition, and workflow generation methods, achieving the highest average accuracy across tasks and models. Programs optimized with the weakest executor transfer to other models without further optimization. This allows them to be deployed in production on inexpensive models, where they reach accuracy comparable to frontier reasoning models. Unlike these monolithic models, SCOPE produces substantially more stable predictions, and each verdict is interpretable through the predicate outputs that produced it. Additionally, we validate the key components of our method through an ablation study. SCOPE thus enables reliable, interpretable, and efficient policy enforcement at production scale.

##### **Diagnosing the Explicit-to-Applied Theory of Mind Gap in LLMs**
2610.31060v1 by Prajvi Saxena, Sabine Janzen, Wolfgang Maass

As large language models are deployed in high-stakes roles such as therapy, education, and personal assistance, their social cognition must be assessed reliably. Current Theory of Mind (ToM) benchmarks report aggregate scores that conflate two distinct competencies: explicit ToM (recognizing what someone believes) and applied ToM (using that belief to predict behavior). Building on this distinction, we investigate the gap and its recoverability using four structurally diverse benchmarks. Aggregate reporting obscures uneven competence: on SimpleToM, Llama-3.1-8B scores 51.5 percent overall while recognizing mental states at 99.4 percent and predicting behavior at only 28.9 percent. To assess recovery, we compare structured prompting, matched in-context learning, and our proposed Belief-State Test-Time Training (BSTTT), which adapts lightweight parameters on answered support questions through action reconstruction. Across Qwen2.5-7B, Llama-3.1-8B, and GPT-oss-20B, recovery depends on the model, task, and intervention. On SimpleToM, reminders combined with BSTTT improve Llama's behavior prediction by 40.8 percentage points; reminders account for most of the overall improvement, with BSTTT adding 1.3 to 5.5 points beyond reminders across models. Contextual examples often outperform weight updates, while BSTTT remains stronger in specific model-benchmark settings. Temporal belief tracking remains difficult despite partial recovery. These findings show that aggregate ToM scores can overstate applied competence while hiding performance recoverable through targeted assistance. We recommend decomposed reporting of explicit and applied accuracy alongside intervention comparisons that establish which failures are recoverable and under what conditions.

##### **Directing to Diversify: Multi-Directional Search for Automatic Heuristic Design**
2610.31048v1 by Jianghan Zhu, Jianan Zhou, Fei Liu, Ni Zhang, Yaoxin Wu, Zhuoyi Lin, Zhiguang Cao

Large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong potential to automate heuristic design for NP-hard combinatorial optimization (CO) tasks without human intervention. Recent methods predominantly rely on evolutionary computation (EC) frameworks, where heuristic diversification is mainly induced through EC-like operators or downstream selection, while the direction of heuristic transformation remains largely unharnessed. In this paper, we propose a multi-directional Perturbation-Rewrite Search (PRS) framework for automatic heuristic design that factorizes heuristic transformation through directional search primitives at the generation level. Specifically, PRS operates on heuristic ideas through three complementary directions: expansion toward richer and more expressive designs, compression toward simpler and less specialized designs, and steering for fine-grained refinement, together enabling broader and more sustained exploration of diverse regions in the heuristic design space. PRS further organizes candidate exploration within a breadth-first search-based process with adaptive operator control, which ensures thorough candidate utilization and sustained exploration, and maintains strong search dynamics while preventing premature convergence. Extensive empirical results across diverse CO tasks show that PRS consistently discovers high-quality heuristics, demonstrating the effectiveness and generality of explicitly directional exploration design.

##### **Harm Is Behavioural, Moderation Is Lexical: How Language and Culture Decide Whom Automated Safety Systems Protect and Whom They Silence**
2610.31047v1 by Gabriel Lars Sabadin, Dr Nicola Harding, Sarah Kingston

Content warning: this paper quotes slurs and hateful statements from published test suites; the racial slur among them is masked.
  Automated systems decide at scale which messages are harmful, and they decide unevenly. Classifiers label African American English as offensive up to twice as often as other English, the accounts of prominent drag queens can score as more toxic than those of white nationalists, and safety behaviour that holds in English collapses in low-resource languages. We argue that over-flagging and under-protection share one cause: deciding harm from vocabulary and surface form rather than from what a message does. We organise the evidence into seven failure modes and argue, as testable hypotheses, that harmful behaviours (a request for secrecy, a demand for payment, an attempt to isolate someone) travel across languages better than the words that express them. Applied to five open systems in eleven languages, from a keyword lexicon to two current multilingual guard models, the protocol finds that none reaches native-speaker accuracy at separating harmful function from harmless form (51 of 55 system-language pairs fall short); that greater capability raises discrimination in nine languages while leaving Arabic and romanised Hindi near chance, so the gap between the best- and worst-served languages grows with capability; that one system both over-flags and under-protects the same language most; and that a language a system reads worst can look the most fairly treated when only false positives are reported.

##### **Conflict-Aware Logit Adapters for Utility-Preserving Anti-Distillation**
2610.31046v1 by Quoc Phong Dao, Binh Anh Nguyen, Linh Ngo Van, Thanh-Toan Do, Mehrtash Harandi, Trung Le

While reasoning traces improve model capability, they also provide supervision signals for downstream distillation. Initial efforts to mitigate this issue rely on decoding-time interventions, but they incur high inference overhead and can harm the teacher model's natural fluency. We propose Conflict-Aware Logit Adapters (CALA), a lightweight training-time defense. CALA attaches a small residual adapter to a frozen teacher model and trains only the adapter. A proxy student identifies positions where imitation is most sensitive, and the adapter perturbs the output distribution at these positions while remaining within high-probability regions. A conflict-aware optimization strategy balances anti-distillation and utility objectives, while regularization preserves the teacher's performance and fluency. At inference time, CALA incurs modest overhead and requires no auxiliary model. Experiments on reasoning benchmarks show that CALA substantially reduces successful student imitation while maintaining near-original teacher performance and generation quality. These results establish CALA as a practical and efficient alternative to decoding-time approaches.

2610.31041v1 by Firuz Kamalov, Hana Sulieman, Ahmed El Sayed, Sivaraj R., Nitesh Sughnani

Generative artificial intelligence promises to democratize personalized tutoring, yet the longitudinal stability of adaptive systems remains under-theorized. Current ethical discourse focuses on static bias. This paper argues that a distinct threat lies in algorithmic drift: a feedback loop in which minor, demographically asymmetric observation errors compound over time into systemic inequities. We make three contributions. First, we formalize algorithmic drift as a coupled learner--tutor dynamical system. Second, we corroborate this theory with an agent-based Monte Carlo simulation in which either a transparent online tracker or an LSTM deep-knowledge-tracing network drives an IRT-based curriculum policy. Third, we introduce the temporal fairness stabilizer (TFS), a targeted intervention that periodically re-probes learner capability, and benchmark it against standard exploration baselines with explicit group-fairness metrics and replication-level inference. Within the modeled environment, TFS closes the achievement gap where generic exploration does not, and we characterize the conditions under which it succeeds. We separate what is established analytically, what is demonstrated in simulation, and what remains to be validated with real learners. Our results reframe algorithmic fairness in education as a temporal property of a closed loop rather than a deployment-time checkbox.

##### **Multichoice Modeling: Exploring Decision Configuration Space under Uncertainty**
2610.31038v1 by Mariia Kozlova, Roman Stepanova, Alexander Walzer, Andrea Saltelli

Decision support has become sophisticated at exploring uncertainty, but much less so at representing the architecture of the decision itself. As a result, the decision space is often narrowed before uncertainty is analyzed, leaving interactions among choices and the effects of alternatives they imply largely invisible. This paper introduces multichoice modeling, a framework for making large decision configuration spaces explicit and computationally explorable under uncertainty. Drawing on the logic of rugged landscapes, we conceptualize such problems as contingent performance landscapes, where interdependent choices generate configuration-specific outcomes whose value changes across uncertain futures. The computational framework combines multidimensional decision and uncertainty spaces in a single simulation. Further, variance-based sensitivity analysis is used for identifying influential choices, and conditional distribution analysis for comparing configuration-specific performance profiles. We demonstrate the framework through three case studies: a manufacturing process upgrade, M&A strategy, and mortgage design. By contrasting conventional uncertainty-only analysis with multichoice modeling, the cases show how opening the decision configuration space can reveal unexpected complementarities, near-best configurations with different risk profiles, and strategies that were not apparent as alternatives at the outset.

#### Abstracts
##### **How Ground Truth Shapes Retrieval Scores: Evidence-Aware Evaluation of a Retrieval-Augmented Generation System for Breast Lesions**
2610.31606v1 by Miguel Mochizuki Silva, Samuel Ismael, Mateus C. Dias, Thaís Gaudêncio, Yuri Malheiros,  Barufaldi

Purpose: To describe an evidence-aware retrieval evaluation protocol for a clinical retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) system over breast lesion management literature, addressing the dependence of retrieval scores on annotation coverage. Methods: An 18-document corpus and an 18-query development benchmark, with one Yes/No or WH-question per document, were used. Retrieval combined dense embeddings, BM25, and cross-encoder reranking. Chunk sizes of 128 to 1024 tokens, overlaps of 0 to 20%, and four retriever configurations were evaluated. Relevance was defined by evidence spans mapped to all containing chunks, treating overlapping chunks with equivalent evidence as relevant. Retrieval was evaluated using R-Precision, Recall@K, MRR, and nDCG@K. Separately, generation was assessed on a 512-token index at K=1 to 5 using DeepEval claim-level metrics, with deepseek-v4-pro as the LLM judge, and BLEU/ROUGE metrics. Results: The selected hybrid retriever, using 384-token chunks and 20% overlap, retrieved accepted evidence in the top three results for 17 of 18 queries. Recall@3, R-Precision, MRR@3, and nDCG@3 each reached 0.94. Ground-truth curation increased evidence spans from 25 to 37 and accepted chunk targets from 36 to 51 without changing retrieval rankings. Conclusion: Cross-encoder reranking achieved the highest retrieval scores. Expanded relevance annotations increased recognized evidence without changing rankings, highlighting annotation sensitivity. The small development set and retrieval-informed curation require independent validation, including radiologist assessment of evidence sufficiency.

摘要：

##### **Would an LLM Match into a Residency? Uncertainty, Reasoning and Image Use of Open Language Models on the Spanish MIR Examination**
2610.31586v1 by Miguel Fernandez-de-Retana, Unai Zulaika, Ruben Sanchez-Corcuera, Aritz Bilbao-Jayo, Aitor Almeida

Accuracy on medical examinations is common evidence offered for the clinical use of language models, yet a health system that intends to run an open model on its own hardware needs to know considerably more: whether the model recognizes when it is likely to be wrong, whether medical fine-tuning improves this ability, whether its correct answers rest on sound clinical reasoning, or whether it actually uses the images it is given. We examine these questions for four open models of 8 to 27 billion parameters on the Spanish MIR residency examination, scoring 1,552 questions from eight editions under the exam's own rules. Answering every question, the three larger models would rank among roughly the first 600 to 3,000 candidates, with 41 to 43 specialties still open to them. Abstention, however, never pays, because even their least confident answers are right more often than the 25% break-even. Medical fine-tuning reduced overconfidence at the cost of accuracy, without improving how well confidence separates right from wrong answers. Moreover, 13-38% of correct answers rejected a distractor for a clinically unsound reason that confidence did not reflect, and removing or swapping the provided contextual images left accuracy unchanged.

摘要：

##### **From Prompt Rules to Golden Data Model: Production-Scale Generation of Personalized, Compliant Pharmaceutical Claims**
2610.31519v1 by Suyash Mishra, Qiang Li, Subhabrata Adak, Upender Phogat, Srikanth Patil, Satyanarayan Pati

Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to generate domain-specific commercial & medical content (e.g., claims, statements, and promotional phrases) in regulated industries such as pharmaceuticals. However, ensuring its regulatory compliance, factual accuracy, and audience-specific personalization remains a major challenge. We report a production case study asking whether moving compliance and personalization signal from prompt-level rules into retrieval time structured context improves pharmaceutical claims generation, using Composer, a deployed internal tool built on a Golden Data Model (GDM) of 1,867 pharmaceutical claims spanning four products. The GDM integrates claim metadata, references, audience labels, material type, usage history, Match Text Variant (MTV) links, and a six dimension quality score (6C). Across six product-audience segments, the context pipeline improves intended indication (+29%), intended content type (+62%) and intended target audience (+67%), 6C quality score (+86%), and claim reuse (+34%). Production log analysis over the deployment period shows a 29.5% reduction in weekly API cost. We report this as descriptive deployment evidence. This work is a systems and data architecture contribution: it shows that in a regulated, high-stakes domain, restructuring claim data for retrieval-time filtering is a more scalable lever than expanding prompt-level rules, and documents the engineering path data cleaning, graph linking, and quality scoring.

摘要：

##### **Major Revision, Minor Accountability: LLMs as Reviewers of Published Articles in Top-Tier Journals**
2610.31414v1 by Bastien Le Guellec, Su Hwan Kim, Raphael Bentegeac, Aghiles Hamroun

With the growth of scientific output, the peer review system is approaching a breaking point: reviewers are increasingly hard to secure, and turnaround times lengthen accordingly. LLM-assisted or LLM-generated peer review has been proposed as a remedy, but how such models would treat manuscripts already published in top-tier journals, the closest thing the current system has to a gold standard of scientific validity, is not known. Here we assembled a corpus of 200 articles, each released under a CC BY 4.0 license and published in 2026, from 20 general-science, life-science, and clinical-medicine journals including Nature, Science, and Cell, and asked four current LLMs (two proprietary, two open-weight) to produce independent peer-review reports of each one under a blinded protocol. Their decisions were overwhelmingly harsh and starkly inconsistent with each other: 605 of 800 reviews (75.6%) recommended major revision or rejection, but the rate at which any single model did so ranged from 17.5% to 100% (p < 0.001), disagreement on manuscripts that had, by construction, already survived real editorial review at the journals in question. When the same manuscripts were doctored with one deliberately planted, field-specific methodological or statistical flaw, detection rates varied from 17.0% to 60.5% across models (p < 0.001). Recommending revision for papers that had already passed editorial review (low specificity) while missing flaws deliberately placed in front of them (low sensitivity), these models produce output that should be read for what it is: an uneven, framing- and model-dependent signal.

摘要：

##### **ColaBench: Evaluating Memory-Guided Knowledge Acquisition in Personal Agents**
2610.30989v1 by Wenjie Zhang, Zhichao Shi, Xuhui Jiang, Xiaojun Wu, Cehao Yang, Chengjin Xu, Xiaoning Song

Personal agents need to draw on long-term interaction history to retrieve and apply external knowledge suited to a user's current request. Even an answer grounded in relevant documents can be unsuitable when it overlooks the user's circumstances. This creates two connected challenges: using historical conditions to identify the knowledge needed for a new request, and applying that knowledge to the user's situation. Existing benchmarks advance memory recall, personalized retrieval, and memory-guided action, while the joint evaluation of these two challenges remains underexplored. We introduce ColaBench, a synthetic benchmark in health and daily living, with 349 tasks across 10 Profiles and a knowledge base of 13,033 documents. Its Evidence Graph connects personal conditions to their disclosures across public interaction sessions, records prior document exposure, and links answer requirements to personal and newly acquired document evidence. We also introduce MemKB, which uses state summaries to inform queries and rule application, and memory-document links to retrieve knowledge associated with the user's history. MemKB achieves 86.25% Accuracy with an average of 4.07 interaction rounds per task. Task-level analysis finds larger gains on tasks integrating information across multiple historical Facts; error analysis further identifies unsupported personal assumptions and rule misapplication as obstacles to using retrieved evidence. ColaBench provides traceable tasks and an evaluation framework for studying how memory guides knowledge acquisition and rule application.

摘要：

##### **RetiJev: Fast and Source-Grounded Ophthalmic Decision Models**
2610.30947v1 by Bo Liu, Lianyu Wang, Meng Wang, Ching-Yu Cheng, Huazhu Fu

Ophthalmic clinical decision support requires fast, accurate, and privacy-preserving inference, yet autoregressive large language models (LLMs) incur substantial latency and computational overhead for decisions among predefined actions. We therefore investigate a decision-oriented alternative to generative reasoning. Specifically, we first introduce RetiJev-Data, a source-grounded ophthalmology corpus comprising 78,158 clinical-decision and diagnosis questions derived from articles in PubMed Central and a MeSH vocabulary covering 531 eye diseases, and 50,074 guideline-grading questions spanning 11 grading systems, along with a 24,662-question evaluation benchmark. Based on this, we develop RetiJev, a family of 0.8B-9B decision models that directly score candidate answers without autoregressive decoding. Across all model sizes, RetiJev outperforms both a 27B LLM and the commercial Jev 1.13 decision model on guideline grading by 10.4-23.0 points. RetiJev-9B further achieves the highest accuracy on clinical decisions, improving over both baselines by 5.4-6.5 points while matching them on diagnosis. It answers a case in 158 ms on one GPU, 3.5x faster than LLM generation and 470x faster than reasoning. Models are released at https://github.com/Awenbocc/RetiJev.

##### **Better Reasoning, Not Just Bigger Models: A Structure-Grounded Framework for Efficient and Reliable Inference**

It also compares a 1-lead configuration using lead II with the corresponding 12-lead configuration. Additional experiments on glucose, hemoglobin, and potassium are presented in the appendix.

The 12-lead configuration consistently achieved better performance. Regression reached a mean absolute error of 534.98 pg/mL for 1-lead and 514.27 pg/mL for 12-lead. Binary classification achieved AUROCs of 0.849 and 0.876, respectively, while three-class classification achieved macro-averaged AUROCs of 0.738 and 0.752.
  The results indicate that precise NT-proBNP regression remains challenging, whereas broader concentration ranges can be classified more effectively. The findings are consistent with the physiological hypothesis that ECG characteristics and NT-proBNP concentrations reflect shared cardiovascular processes. ECG-based NT-proBNP prediction may therefore provide complementary information, particularly for classifying broader concentration ranges. A self-correction experiment is consistent with this: shown its own flagged errors, every model except Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct reduces hallucination; the one model for which collaboration lowers hallucination gains the most, and Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct, the model whose hallucination it amplifies most, does not reduce its hallucination at all. We find that collaboration redistributes what its models can already do; it does not supply what they cannot.

Reinforcement learning for language models has moved from preference reward models to verifiable rewards, which sustain much longer optimization. Preference models are prone to reward hacking: the policy raises their score without improving response quality, or even while degrading it. Yet for open-ended tasks, where quality can neither be verified nor fully written down as rubrics, they remain valuable. We introduce Dynalize, which mitigates reward hacking online, during policy training. Periodically and asynchronously, a language model analyzes training rollouts and writes penalties for undesirable behaviors that the reward model appears to reward; a judge applies them to every rollout. In clinical counseling and creative writing, training on the reward model alone raises its score continuously but ultimately leads to a decline in external benchmarks; Dynalize sustains longer training and results in improved downstream performance. We find that our method suppresses patterns incentivized by preference models that, without penalties, spread to most responses, and even to the model's reasoning, which the reward model does not explicitly score. Clinical decision-making often begins with underspecified information and requires not only gathering missing information but also knowing when to stop. This calls for judging determinability (whether the available information is sufficient for a decision) by considering whether missing information could change the conclusion, not simply the amount of available decision-relevant information. Reliance on this amount as a cue for determinability, which we term \textit{factor coverage bias}, could undermine the trustworthiness of large language models (LLMs) through premature conclusions or excessive information gathering, such as unnecessary invasive tests. Yet whether LLMs exhibit this bias remains insufficiently studied. To address this gap, we construct a controlled pairwise benchmark in which LLMs identify the determinable case within each determinable--undeterminable pair. We add a decision-relevant factor to either side while keeping each case's determinability unchanged, and similarly add irrelevant text to distinguish this bias from context-length effects. Adding a relevant factor made LLMs more likely to select the augmented case as determinable even when it remained undeterminable, an effect that persisted with increased reasoning effort, while adding irrelevant text made them less likely to select the augmented case, an effect that reasoning substantially reduced. These findings identify factor coverage bias as a failure mode distinct from context-length effects and a potential risk to LLM trustworthiness. Our work provides an evaluation framework for LLM trustworthiness under underspecification.

##### **A Calibrated Instrument for Detecting Disposition Drift in Language Models**
2610.25735v1 by Jerry Kaplan

Before language models are released, they are tested for harmful traits such as willingness to carry out dangerous requests, deception, power seeking, and sycophancy. The safe behavior these tests certify may degrade as models are modified, by topical fine-tuning, new training data, or changes to settings such as the depth of their reasoning. Most work reports such drift as single summary rates scored by another AI model, without the error analysis needed to interpret them. We adapt a calibrated measurement methodology recently developed for assessing how inference optimizations affect the quality of model outputs. Applying it to several misalignment dimensions in two open-weight models, we first establish the instrument's precision, showing that shifts of a few percentage points can be reliably detected. We then induce drift deliberately by fine-tuning on correct and on subtly harmful medical and financial advice; harmful advice induces a far greater shift than correct advice. Most strikingly, a model that reasons before answering can conceal the shift almost entirely in its answers, even though a direct reading of its internal state shows that its disposition has moved. With reasoning switched off, the same model shifts steadily as the share of harmful advice in its training grows. These results demonstrate the importance of testing safety with reasoning turned both on and off.

2610.25712v1 by Qihan Huang, Lingxia Zhang, Guye Zhang, Zizhu Wang, Xiaowei Mao

Compatible projections reuse these bases, while a globally shared full core preserves the flexibility of each projection. The multi-head design further captures complementary deformation patterns, ensuring both global alignment and local precision. (1) Label-space restriction - an evaluation-rule change, not a retraining gain - is the dominant correction (73.76%->79.74% on TCGA-BRCA; 63.16%->68.42% on GSE21653); (2) EM prior correction on the reconciled space is an optional calibration step (Brier fidelity up, macro F1 down; to 80.65% and 74.06%); and (3) the target label space is not recoverable from unlabelled predictions (EM retains prior 0.0751 on the absent class; calibrated detectors fail), so compliance must come from cohort documentation. The protocol exposes why internal validation overstates cross-cohort value: multimodal fusion gains do not transfer (no configuration beats mRNA alone, n=694; McNemar p=0.13-1.00), and the genomic panel adds nothing to pathological complete response prediction over clinical variables (DeltaAUC = +0.0045). Clinically, without label-space compliance, cross-site accuracy comparisons misattribute annotation-standard differences to model skill; Luminal A/B and claudin-low boundary errors map to distinct treatment decisions; and subtype accuracy is a weaker portable endpoint than survival discrimination (external C-index 0.577-0.584). Reporting the cohort's allowed label set and applying label-space compliance are therefore prerequisites before any cross-cohort performance claim.

Meanwhile, the medical literature offers a wealth of high-quality unlabeled documents, including clinical case reports that trace the full clinical course from initial presentation to final treatment decisions. We study whether document-grounded self-play can convert these reports into effective RL training signals. We identify two challenges that limit existing self-play methods in this setting: knowledge-point fixation}, where the proposer repeatedly asks about a narrow set of salient clues in a source report, and difficulty-quality mismatch, where appropriately difficult questions may still be poorly formed or shallow, limiting their value for reasoner training. To address these challenges, we propose CasePlay, a self-play framework with two key components: (a) a Knowledge-Conditioned Proposer, which anchors question generation to case-specific knowledge points so that questions draw on a broader range of evidence from the source report; and (b) a Rubric-Judge Reasoner, which augments answer-accuracy feedback with rubric-based quality feedback on clue integration, reasoning depth, and option quality, guiding the proposer toward reasoning-intensive questions that better support reasoner training. Extensive experiments across seven medically related benchmarks demonstrate that CasePlay outperforms existing self-play baselines and improves the SFT warm-start model by 3.1 points on average, highlighted by a 6.3-point gain on LiveClin. ##### **From Prediction to Action: Population Health World Model for Digital Medicine**
2610.24662v1 by Xike Xie, Yu Li, Weizhong Yang, Amalie Dyda, Shaohua Kevin Zhou

Digital medicine now captures data that traces individual health trajectories and reveals population health dynamics through electronic health records, claims, remote monitoring, wearable devices, mobile devices, social sensing data, medical images, etc. But much of its modeling still centers on task-specific, outcome-specific, and prediction-specific estimation. The next frontier is not simply better prediction, but action in an evolving health world shaped by multiple decision-makers, heterogeneous populations, resource constraints, and feedback in care delivery. This is why digital medicine now needs world models: not only to estimate what will happen, but to represent how actions change what can happen next -- whom to prioritize, how to allocate scarce resources, how to adapt care across subgroups, and how interventions reshape future health trajectories and population dynamics.
  We call such a model population health world model (PHWM): a more foundational representation of an evolving health world in which health states, individual trajectories, population health dynamics, interventions, constraints, and feedback are intrinsically coupled. Building a PHWM endows three unmet structural gaps in current digital medicine: dynamic health-state modeling, population-level intervention intelligence, and validation, equity, and governance for action-oriented use. We argue that addressing these gaps requires three minimal capabilities: representation learning under action-dependent observation, multi-scale intervention intelligence, and validation with explicit decision boundaries, while highlighting the importance of social, contextual, and public-health sensing data for action-oriented digital medicine.

Sycophancy, manipulation, and privacy loss provide a case study of how these risks may interact and accumulate. Longitudinal electronic health records (EHRs) contain complementary diagnostic and laboratory information about patient health. However, irregularity, sparsity, heterogeneity, and incompleteness in documentation complicate EHR modeling. To address the problem, we introduce GraphBEAT (Graph-based Bucketized Event Aggregation over Time), a temporal graph representation learning framework that groups clinical events into time buckets and transforms observed features into patient-specific, temporally directed graphs to enable early risk stratification for gastrointestinal cancers. Each node in an EHR graph encodes feature identity, observed value, and temporal position of clinical events. A context-aware, time-decay message-passing mechanism models transitions between successive buckets, followed by multi-head attention pooling to generate patient-level representations. GraphBEAT was trained and evaluated on multi-site longitudinal diagnosis codes and laboratory tests to predict pancreatic, liver, and intestinal cancer at 12-, 24-, and 36-month diagnostic lead times. In all experimental setups, GraphBEAT consistently outperformed baseline graph neural networks (GraphSAGE, GAT, and GCN) and sequence modeling approaches (Transformer and long short-term memory [LSTM]). GraphBEAT also required over 250x fewer floating-point operations per patient and over 20x fewer parameters than the Transformer and LSTM baselines. These results support GraphBEAT as an accurate and computationally efficient approach for cancer risk stratification from sparse, heterogeneous longitudinal health records.

摘要：

##### **spora: A Unified Multimodal Dataset and Platform for Spatial Proteomics**
2610.24002v1 by Benedikt von Querfurth, Johann Wenckstern, Eeshaan Jain, Lukas Klein, Phil Cheng, Yexiang Cheng, Cedric Vincent-Cuaz, Martina Haberecker, Pascal Frossard, Andreas Wicki, Olivier Michielin, Charlotte Bunne

Spatial proteomics (SP) captures the molecular composition and spatial organization of tissues at single-cell resolution, opening a direct view of the cellular ecosystems that drive disease. Protein panels, acquisition platforms, and tissue contexts differ from one cohort to the next, and a coherent picture of tissue biology can only emerge from models trained to bridge this heterogeneity. Foundation models offer the natural path forward, but their training depends on large, harmonized corpora that span platforms, panels, and cohorts. No public resource currently approaches that scale; available data sit in small, narrowly scoped releases with incompatible formats and conventions. We present $\texttt{spora}$, a large-scale multimodal SP dataset of 13,546 standardized multiplex images from 5,477 patients across 35 cohorts and five major SP technologies. Curated with biomedical experts, $\texttt{spora}$ provides model training-ready formats together with cell and tissue segmentations, structured clinical metadata, and, where available, paired H&E enabling the training of virtual staining models. It is complemented by $\texttt{spora [io]}$, a unified interface for tiling, sampling, and standardization across all cohorts and modalities, and $\texttt{spora [bench]}$, a benchmark suite spanning cell phenotyping, diagnostic and therapeutic prediction as well as virtual staining tasks across twelve cohorts that places current SP foundation models on common ground against task specific baselines.

摘要：

##### **The Self-Improvement Problem**
2610.21646v1 by Brian B. Moser, Stanislav Frolov, Tobias C. Nauen, Federico Raue, Andreas Dengel

With the rise of large language models, agentic systems are becoming increasingly autonomous, as they write code, call tools, revise prompts, and propose modifications to their own components. This autonomy raises a fundamental question for safe self-improvement: can an automated gate like a reward model or an automated reviewer reliably decide whether a proposed update should be accepted? The difficulty is structural. The gate must be a terminating, computable procedure, while the property we ultimately care about, namely whether an update is a genuine semantic improvement, depends on potentially unbounded program behavior. We formalize this mismatch by modeling agents as programs and defining improvement in terms of a semantic score that need not be computable. We study two evaluation abstractions. The first is a certifier that approves or rejects an update. The second is a search procedure that, given access to a proposal generator, attempts to output some improving candidate if one exists. We show that, in the fully general setting, no terminating procedure can simultaneously guarantee reliability (never accepting a non-improvement) and non-conservatism (never missing an available improvement): self-referential constructions can force any such gate or searcher to fail. Randomized gates with bounded error face the same limit. We then identify a standard decidable regime used in practice (bounded evaluation and a finite proposal budget) under which exhaustive simulation yields a terminating search procedure with the desired guarantees. We also show the limit of this recovery: a chain of accepted updates can raise the bounded score in every round while the true score falls. Our results draw a boundary between unbounded, universal settings where correctness guarantees collapse and bounded settings where they can be recovered by design.

摘要：

##### **Agent Status Protocol (ASP): A Lightweight Specification for State Reporting in Multi-Agent Systems**
2610.21538v1 by Diego Gosmar, Deborah A. Dahl, Emmett Coin, Csaba Bolyós, Andreas Zettl

The Agent Status Protocol (ASP) defines a lightweight way for heterogeneous AI agents to report their operational state to a status server, and for observers such as dashboards to receive that state as soon as it changes. Agents push small JSON events over HTTP POST; the server exposes the aggregated state to observers over Server-Sent Events (SSE). Because an agent that has crashed, hung or been compromised cannot be trusted to report its own failure, ASP does not rely on self-reports alone: the server infers liveness from missed heartbeats, can detect agents that are alive but no longer making progress, and accepts observations from supervisors, guardrails, operators and peers, some of which take precedence over what the agent says about itself while others are shown alongside it. ASP models state along independent dimensions (lifecycle, health, task, floor, governance), so that an agent can be, for example, online, degraded, working on a task and waiting for the floor at the same time. It reuses existing vocabularies: task states from the Agent2Agent (A2A) protocol, floor states from the Open Floor Protocol (OFP), and attribute conventions from the OpenTelemetry GenAI semantic conventions. This paper presents version 0.1 of ASP as a proposal, not as an approved standard. It is a working draft intended to open a discussion, and the authors invite comments, alternative proposals and independent implementations from the community.

摘要：

##### **Tool-Augmented Large Language Models for Reliable Medical Calculation from Patient Notes**
2610.21477v1 by Yang Zhong, Ling Luo, Xuanlyu Zhu, Yanhua Wang, Yuanyuan Sun, Jian Wang, Hongfei Lin

Medical calculators translate raw patient observations into clinically actionable values and scores. However, applying these calculators directly to free-form patient notes requires precise variable extraction combined with the exact execution of complex formulas, scoring rules, and output conventions. Relying solely on direct large language model (LLM) prompting is unreliable in this context, as rule recall, parameter extraction, arithmetic reasoning, and formatting become entangled within a single generative step. To address these limitations, we propose a tool-augmented LLM framework for reliable medical calculation from patient notes. The framework retrieves and structures calculator specifications, generates reusable executable tools, and uses a constrained extractor to map each patient note to calculator-ready inputs. The tools then perform deterministic calculation, separating clinical language understanding from rule execution and arithmetic. Evaluated on the MedCalc-Bench dataset, our method achieves 86.73% final-answer accuracy, outperforming the one-shot prompting and supervised fine tuning-only baselines by 24.0 and 17.27 percentage points. Ablation and error analyses show that these performance gains are primarily driven by rule-grounded tool synthesis and schema-constrained extraction, whereas remaining failures are concentrated in clinical-semantic extraction and benchmark-specific output contracts. By grounding calculator logic in executable tools, the proposed approach alleviates arithmetic hallucinations and improves the computational reliability of the final clinical outputs.

摘要：

##### **Ontology Refinement with Embeddings and LLMs: A Case Study on CASTON**
2610.21466v1 by Hui Yang, Ziyu Li, Xuan Liu, Anders Jarfors, He Tan, Jiaoyan Chen

Ontologies are widely used to represent and organize complex knowledge in domains such as biomedicine and healthcare. However, constructing reliable ontologies remains challenging, as class hierarchies, whether manually or automatically constructed, may contain subtle subsumption errors that require careful refinement. We study this problem in CASTON, a casting-domain ontology, and present a lightweight but scalable refinement workflow that combines a fine-tuned ontology embedding model with Large Language Models (LLMs). The embedding model is used to extract a limited number of candidate axioms for refinement together with possible replacement candidates, and an LLM then returns repair suggestions. In a small expert evaluation, the default configuration using a CASTON-fine-tuned OnT embedding model and Qwen3-8B produced reliable high-confidence repairs: all six high-confidence replacements were accepted by both domain experts. The implementation is available at https://github.com/HuiYang1997/OntRefine.

摘要：

##### **Incentivizing Tool-Use Reinforcement Learning for Post-Arrival Emergency Care Prediction**
2610.21449v2 by Pengfei Hu, Chenwei Wu, Qiao Jin, Jun Deng, Zhiyong Lu, Liyue Shen, Yue Ning

Large language models (LLMs) can reason over both structured measurements and free-text records, which makes them attractive for emergency department (ED) prediction. However, most existing LLMs still fall short of conventional ML methods like trees on EHR-based prediction tasks. Our controlled analysis traces this gap to two weaknesses: LLMs do not select informative features, and they misjudge what numerical values imply for risk even when given the right features. We propose TIR-EC, a tool-integrated reinforcement learning framework that addresses both. TIR-EC exposes EHR feature groups as tools the LLM can call. It trains the LLM with rewards from a gradient-boosted tree teacher, which provides a reference set of informative tools and a graded risk target. TIR-EC also distills task-specific clinical skills into the model weights through a reverse curriculum that gradually removes the skills from the prompt during RL process. Evaluation on three clinical prediction tasks shows that our 4B agent achieves the highest macro F1 of all compared models, outperforms conventional ML baselines, and is competitive with much larger frontier LLMs. Further analysis shows that TIR-EC selects more useful evidence with fewer tool calls and combines measurements more effectively.

摘要：

##### **TRACER: A Multimodal Harness for Patient Care in Hospitals**
2610.21266v1 by Zane Durante, Paul Tang, Keshigeyan Chandrasegaran, Amit Kaushal, Shrinidhi Kowshika Lakshmikanth, Feolu Kolawole, Yu Jun Zheng, Yifan Geng, Shobhit Agarwal, Kevin Schulman, Juan Carlos Niebles, Ehsan Adeli, Arnold Milstein, Li Fei-Fei

Continuous video and clinical records provide complementary information for understanding how patient behavior evolves over a hospital stay. A central challenge is how to represent behavior across the entire stay while preserving access to fine-grained visual and clinical evidence. We introduce TRACER, a training-free multimodal harness that constructs minute-level action records from video and combines them with clinical records into hourly reports. The harness equips an LLM agent with tools to query this hierarchy, inspect relevant video, and retrieve clinical records. To evaluate TRACER, we construct two benchmarks. The first contains 397 annotated one-minute clips spanning 15 atomic actions; the second poses 144 clinician-informed multiple-choice questions, grounded in 264 hours of video and clinical records from two ICU patients. TRACER achieves up to 84.8% accuracy for action detection and 61.0% QA accuracy versus 49.3% for the strongest baseline using the same backbone. Across 280 patients spanning approximately 60,000 camera-hours, TRACER identifies significantly less purposeful activity in patients with a positive delirium assessment during their stay than in those without (p < 0.0001). Our results show that combining continuous video with clinical records can support understanding of patient behavior over time and across patient cohorts without training on protected health data.

摘要：

##### **Disentangling Depression from Cognitive Decline in Elderly Speech Using Concurrent Clinical Assessments**
2610.26890v1 by Woori Jeon, Seunghee Ha, Sang-Kyu Lee, Ji Hye Yoon, Tae-Jin Yoon, Seung Jin Lee, Woojae Han, Jungmin So

Detecting depression from speech in elderly patients with mild cognitive impairment (MCI) is complicated by overlapping acoustic effects of cognitive decline. Without disentangling these, classifiers risk learning cognitive rather than depression-specific patterns. We present a Korean elderly speech corpus of 89 MCI speakers collected over three years with concurrent depression (SGDS) and cognitive (MMSE) assessments. Both linear mixed-effects models and partial correlations identify the same pattern: of seven feature groups, only formant features are associated with depression after controlling for cognitive function, while widely used F0 features carry no signal. The F1+F2 subset achieves UAR 0.760 with just 12 features, outperforming both the full eGeMAPS baseline and self-supervised representations. These findings show that concurrent clinical assessments can identify interpretable, depression-specific acoustic markers in populations where cognitive and affective symptoms co-occur.

#### Abstracts
##### **Causal Chain of Thought: Mitigating Attention Leakage via Context Isolation**
2610.31639v1 by Gabriele Dominici, Teague McMillan, Fazl Barez, Marc Langheinrich, Martin Gjoreski

Chain-of-thought (CoT) prompting elicits step-by-step rationales that appear to explain model predictions, but there is no guarantee these steps causally drive the final answer. In standard autoregressive generation, dense attention allows each reasoning step to attend to the full context, enabling the model to derive answers through direct lookup while generating plausible-sounding but post-hoc justifications. We introduce CausalCoT, an inference framework that enforces causal transparency by decomposing problems into a directed acyclic graph of atomic sub-questions, evaluating each node in an isolated context containing only its direct causal parents. This Markovian structure removes attention pathways to non-parent content, making downstream predictions sensitive to local reasoning rather than global context leakage. Across a diverse suite of reasoning benchmarks, CausalCoT preserves competitive predictive performance while improving explanation faithfulness, increasing causal sensitivity of intermediate reasoning steps by an average of 12 and up to 21 percentage points over the strongest baseline. The structured dependency graph additionally enables test-time interventions in which local corrections propagate predictably through downstream reasoning.

摘要：

##### **Questioning Instrumental Convergence in RLVR: Checkpoint Evidence from OLMo-3**
2610.31624v1 by Barış Alparslan, Sandy Tanwisuth, Caleb Petterson

Instrumental convergence is often invoked as a general reason to expect capable agents to acquire or express power-seeking subgoals: self-preservation, resource acquisition, deception, or resistance to oversight which poses an important threat to the society. For LLMs, reinforcement learning is the first stage of post-training that directly optimizes behavior against an objective; therefore, it is the stage where convergent instrumental behavior, on IC grounds, likely to appear. Does reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) make a model more likely to express instrumental-convergence behavior, or does this depend on the training regime, evaluation frame, and stage of post-training? We study this question as a checkpoint-conditioned behavioral trajectory rather than as a single before/after safety score. Using InstrumentalEval, we evaluate OLMo-3-7B-Think across SFT, DPO, 13 intermediate RLVR checkpoints, and the final RLVR model. The trajectory is nonmonotonic: instrumental rate rises from 25.0% at SFT to 46.1% near RLVR step 200, then falls to 11.8% at the final checkpoint. Paired item-level tests and step-wise regression support the final SFT-to-RLVR reduction. Prompt framing does not explain this pattern: a harmful-prompt indicator combined with other framing effects explains only 15% of within-checkpoint score variance on average, and Iterative Null-space Projection (INLP) removes a detectable framing direction without changing the SFT-to-final-RLVR gap. Together with preliminary 32B endpoint results (35.53% SFT to 22.37% RLVR final), these findings indicate that RLVR does not monotonically increase instrumental behavior in this setting: the rate rises early, then falls below the SFT baseline. The result is specific to this model family and benchmark, and judge agreement is moderate, so absolute rates should be read cautiously.

摘要：

##### **Simulated Control Evals**
2610.31593v1 by Jeffrey Olmo, David Zhang, Tyler Tracy

Control evaluations measure whether safeguards can prevent an AI model from causing undesired outcomes even when it is intentionally trying to do so. These evaluations typically use purpose-built control settings and extrapolate their results to a model's deployment. However, the effectiveness of safeguards in deployment can depend on features of environments and tasks that control settings do not represent. To broaden where control evaluations can be run, we introduce simulated control evaluations (SCEs), which use a language model to simulate tool responses in place of an environment. We evaluate SCEs on LinuxArena, where execution provides ground-truth outcomes. We compare SCE prediction errors with those from a separate control setting, BashArena, and with variation in control metrics within LinuxArena. For independently sampled evaluation and deployment task sets, SCEs predict safety with a root mean squared error of 3.6 percentage points across five monitors at a 5% audit budget, compared with 10.1 for BashArena. SCEs closely reproduce distributions of attack strategies, attacker reasoning, and monitor reasoning, with over tenfold lower KL divergence from execution than BashArena. SCEs explain 78-85% of the between-environment variance in attack success, monitor scores, and safety. This accuracy is sample efficient: SCEs predict safety with less error than BashArena using fewer than 5% as many task-pair draws as the deployment task set. Strategies discovered through simulation also improve monitor performance on held-out LinuxArena attacks, outperforming BashArena and approximately matching improvement from executed strategies. These results suggest that deployment-specific SCEs can be more predictive than control evaluations executed in separate control settings.

摘要：

##### **FairFRESH: A Neuro-Symbolic Framework for Fair and Explainable Household Food Recommendation**
2610.31506v1 by Kodur Sai Vinay Sathvik, Kakoli Bora, Tejaswini R. M

Recommender systems typically serve one user at a time. However, in households, members share a finite pantry and follow different dietary rules, making independent recommendations prone to resource conflicts and dietary violations. We formulate household food recommendation as constrained fair division and present FairFRESH, a neuro-symbolic framework that learns per-member utilities, allocates foods under explicit welfare objectives, enforces dietary rules through a deterministic filter, and explains recommendations using Shapley and Banzhaf attributions. Theoretically, we show that no finite penalty guarantees compliance near a dietary-rule threshold, establish approximation guarantees for greedy allocation under utilitarian and smoothed Nash objectives in the stock-free model before filtering, and characterize the scale invariance of Nash welfare. We also use the minimax theorem to analyze rotating shared meals. On NHANES-derived data, the learned utility model achieves Recall@20 of 0.293, outperforming five learned recommenders (at most 0.244) but not a previous-day repetition heuristic (0.321). On held-out four-person households, Nash allocation reduces Gini disparity by 42% relative to conflict resolution while improving Recall@20 from 0.180 to 0.197. It comes within 2% of an upper bound on the smoothed Nash optimum, and rescaling one member's utilities changes at most 1.5% of assignments. All recommendations satisfy the encoded dietary rules, while attribution scores correlate with utility loss upon component removal (r = 0.76). In simulation, shared meals reduce pantry use by about one-third, and weekly rotation increases the worst-off member's utility by 38% over the best fixed meal.

##### **When More Metadata Misleads: Probing Field-Level Grounding in LLM-Generated Museum Descriptions**
2610.31193v1 by Clemencia Siro, Laura Hollink

Metadata-conditioned generation is often framed as a data-to-text task in which additional input fields should improve grounding. Museum description generation challenges this assumption because catalogue records are heterogeneous, partially visual, and unevenly documented across geographic regions. We study how metadata composition shapes the generation of artefact descriptions through a two-phase framework. Phase 1 analyses natural metadata variation in a large museum collection, where field count, field type, image availability, and Place co-vary. Phase 2 introduces controlled field probes that hold object identity fixed while varying metadata quantity, field type, and image access. Our results show that metadata quantity alone does not explain generation behaviour. Field type determines whether models ground, omit, or infer information, while images can both support visual grounding and increase unsupported interpretation. More broadly, our findings show that metadata-conditioned data-to-text evaluation should assess field- and claim-level support, rather than relying only on aggregate input coverage or reference similarity.

##### **Zero-Shot Forecasting of In-Situ Soil Moisture and Temperature with a Time-Series Foundation Model**
2610.30971v1 by Mohammad Talaei, Mohammad Goudarzi, Maille Todd, Vanessa N. L. Wong, Iadine Chadès

Soil moisture and temperature govern plant-water availability and microbial activity, making them key indicators for evaluating land-restoration efforts on degraded landscapes. However, the physical and data-driven models normally used to forecast them both need information that a newly instrumented restoration site does not yet have: hydraulic parameters for the former and months of per-site training data for the latter. We evaluate TimesFM 1, a 500M-parameter time-series foundation model, zero-shot on 15-minute telemetry from 27 sensor stations across three land-management regimes (Control, Treatment, Scald) at a semi-arid restoration site, forecasting 5 cm and 30 cm soil moisture and enclosure temperature at seven lead times from 1 h to 168 h. Using gap-aware rolling-origin evaluation across sensors, we find that TimesFM beats the best rule-based statistical forecasting baselines (persistence, seasonal-naive, or 7-day climatology) by 21-76% at sub-daily lead times but loses by 6-79% beyond a day, a pattern explained by a diurnal-alignment mechanism that holds for both soil-moisture depths and temperature. A CatBoost baseline trained walk-forward on the same origins with full history and weather covariates never wins overall, and we show that its weakness is partly a cold-start effect specific to short deployments. We additionally quantify the inference cost of zero-shot forecasting on commodity CPU hardware. Our results highlight a practical trade-off between zero-shot forecasting capability, predictive performance, and computational efficiency.

##### **Measuring the Reasoning-Action Coupling in Driving Vision-Language-Action Models through Counterfactuals**
2610.30953v1 by Gabriel Manalu, Saad Wazir, Thao Do, Seongah Kim, Hyeontaek Hwang, Tolcha Yalew Kidane, Daeyoung Kim

Driving vision-language-action (VLA) models bring an appealing form of explainability to a safety-critical domain, since a system that can state the reasons for its maneuvers offers the transparency that deployment and public trust in autonomous driving demand. These models read the world into language before they act on it, describing the scene and reasoning over it in text that ranges from chain-of-thought to multi-stage question answering on the way to a trajectory. That framing quietly assumes two separable properties, namely that the trajectory follows the generated reasoning and that the generated reasoning tracks the causes that actually produce the trajectory. To thoroughly investigate these properties, we apply causal, counterfactual interventions to four driving VLAs across different benchmarks. First, we perturb the models' own reasoning and show that altering its meaning produces small trajectory changes relative to blanking the same models' camera inputs, a pattern that persists across benchmarks and model architectures. We then construct scene-level counterfactuals through photorealistic removal of key objects named in the models' reasoning and analyze whether the generated reasoning and trajectories track these changes. Overall, our findings indicate that the reasoning these models present to account for their resulting driving behavior is weakly coupled to that behavior itself, pointing to limits on the explainability these systems are intended to provide.

##### **What Lies Beyond Perception: A Partition Theory of the Limits of AI Evaluation and Oversight**
2610.30803v1 by Ahmed M. Mahran

Replacing the judge can move a benchmark score, and models can be trained to hide capabilities. Both put the evaluator in question: what can it certify? We develop a partition theory of evaluation from one principle: judgement is bounded by perception. An agent's discrimination is the partition of its inputs into classes it cannot tell apart. Discrimination is necessary for capability but not sufficient, so bounds on it bear directly on whether evaluation can show that a model lacks an ability. We prove a one-sided bound. An examinee whose partition refines its evaluator's has the discrimination to pass every test the evaluator can grade, provided each input has an acceptable reply. We define the echo test, which accepts any reply the evaluator perceives as identical to the input, and show that it is complete for the tests the evaluator can grade. Passing therefore certifies at most a floor on discrimination, never a ceiling, and only a sharper evaluator can raise it. Used to detect concealment, echo-test failure points the wrong way: under exhaustive probing it flags every honestly weaker examinee and no concealer that presents as the evaluator's peer. Exact imitation of a weaker model is undetectable by any behavioural evaluator. We extend the bound to ensembles of monitors and to channels a model controls, explain published findings on judge disagreement and sandbagging, and derive testable predictions. A safety case that argues from behavioural evaluation alone that a model lacks an ability must assume the model's cooperation.

摘要：

##### **AgentTriage: Replayable Security Diagnosis Across Heterogeneous Agent Runtimes**
2610.30738v1 by Jie Ying, Xutao Zhang, Ningyuan Chen, Mengce Zheng, Jun Li, Zhongjie Zhu, Tiantian Zhu

When tool-use agents write files, execute code, or send data, their final responses do not explain the resulting security events, and heterogeneous runtimes expose the relevant evidence through incompatible interfaces. We present AgentTriage, an executable contract for replayable security diagnosis across heterogeneous runtimes. The contract admits conclusions whose claims cite canonical runtime events and requires abstention when mandatory evidence is missing. Runtime overlays normalize native observations, while a deterministic pipeline joins invocation and result lifecycles, constructs a typed provenance graph, selects a policy-decisive slice, and binds claims to recorded evidence. Given the same normalized trace, overlay version, and pre-run policy oracle, the diagnosis can be recomputed and its claims re-checked without language-model judgment. Across five runtimes, one contract captures all 159 applicable executions, with an audit of native and synthesized identifiers. On 120 adjudicated cases, AgentTriage achieves 0.858 grounded joint diagnosis, 0.966 required-evidence coverage, and a 0.036 unsupported-claim rate, improving joint diagnosis by 0.175 (95% CI [0.092, 0.258]) over the strongest same-schema baseline. These results quantify the precision of replayable diagnosis and the observation boundaries that require abstention. We release an open-source implementation and selected sanitized evidence at https://github.com/AsnGs/agenttriage-open-source.

##### **From Clinical Judgment to Checkable Rules: Clinician-Led Guardrails for Mental-Health Chatbots**
2610.30690v1 by Chenhan Lyu, Kit Wislocki, Sarah Elizabeth Stoeckl, Maitreyi Sinha, Amir Rahmani

Large language models (LLMs) deployed in mental-health contexts can be evaluated for safety failures, but the resulting judgments are often difficult to inspect or explain. Common approaches to improving safety rely on prompt engineering, LLM-as-a-Judge, and Constitutional AI. In this work, we describe a clinician-led alternative in which two clinicians define, finalize, and adjudicate the four safety checks of a rule base, which is formally verified for internal consistency and applied as a guardrail over LLM outputs. Here and in the title, clinician-led refers to this rule base, not to the fact extractor or the uncertainty default, which are ours. Every verdict of the resulting verification layer is recomputable from a named rule and sixteen facts. Against an LLM judge matched in model, revision, quantization, rule text, and decoding but without the layer's computed 988/911 fact, the largest disagreement class, the satisfied-rule class, is structured: the Llama-3.3-70B judge, on its primary prompt, demands a crisis resource from 55 of the 65 replies (84.6%) that already contain one, while the same model, asked as a fact extractor, reports a recognized resource present on 54 of the 55. The verification layer never raises that demand, by construction. We report with equal prominence where this judge is stronger: fewer false crisis alarms on the one reference-scored check. We also report the measured cost (a specification revision raised block-class outcomes on held-out boundary prompts of an over-refusal benchmark from 6.0% to 16.0%, chiefly by changing fact extraction), the clinicians' decision to keep our uncertainty default, and the analysis of the result. The contribution is not a safer verdict. It is a guardrail whose disagreements with the judge localize to named rules and facts and whose measured costs trace to named facts and the uncertainty default.

##### **Automated screening of privacy risks in medical AI**
2610.30229v1 by Konstantinos Vrettos, Eleftherios Tzanis, Haridimos Kondylakis, Evangelia E. Vassalou, Florence X. Doo, Michail E. Klontzas

Medical artificial intelligence (AI) models are often shared without their training data, assuming this protects the patients who contributed them. However, a model can reveal whether a patient's records were used in training, a privacy breach known as a membership inference attack. This risk is usually reported as a cohort average, which can appear negligible while individual patients are almost certainly exposed. Identifying potentially vulnerable records before developing a production model could make privacy assessment more practical. We measured each patient's vulnerability to membership inference attack, across 7,789 models trained on six clinical datasets in four modalities, including two adapted pretrained medical models. Training choices changed how many patients had high attack scores, but the patients who ranked highest by risk largely stayed the same across the configurations we tested. Stronger weight decay reduced the exposed cohort up to threefold, yet largely the same patients remained most at risk across training recipes, architectures and initializations, including foundation models. Therefore, we developed a lightweight screening tool that uses four small models to identify patients with high risk of exposure before production-model training. We also assessed if flagged patients could be protected without removing their data. Privacy screening should become incorporated into data preparation for clinical AI.

摘要：

##### **Keeping Time vs. Understanding Time: Neither Alone Explains Temporal Reasoning in LLMs**
2610.29507v1 by Auss Abbood, Fernando Martínez-Plumed, José Hernández-Orallo, Nigel Collier

Temporal reasoning is widely assumed to decompose into two modes: temporal arithmetic (date and time calculations), and temporal semantics (understanding temporal and causal relations). This dichotomy shapes how benchmarks are designed, how models are trained, and how progress is measured, yet its empirical validity has never been directly tested. We provide two lines of evidence that challenge it. First, we test whether tasks labelled as arithmetic or semantic by the benchmarks support transferable gains within their purported mode. Finetuning on these subsets yields predominantly dataset-specific gains ($+6.8$ to $+23.1$ percentage points), while transfer to a different dataset in the same mode ranges from $-3.2$ to $+1.1$ percentage points. Across the two benchmark families used in the transfer analysis, this pattern provides little evidence of coherent, transferable temporal abilities. Second, using cognitive-demand-based model evaluation, we examine which capabilities actually drive performance on these benchmarks. We find that quantitative and logical reasoning, the assumed proxies for temporal arithmetic and temporal semantics, are not the primary drivers; knowledge is. Together, these findings show that current temporal reasoning benchmarks do not cleanly realise the arithmetic-semantics distinction and instead largely reward models for remembering how time typically works.
